## Covered, flagged (156 rows)

Decision 12: Sonnet read the pair one below the cut-off and Opus at or above it, so the candidate is covered by that trait and flagged for review (Roger sided with Opus on 45 of 58 such pairs in the pilot).

- **acclaimed** (`acclaimed#1`, cut-off 3) by [famous](../../../traits/instructions/famous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Acclaimed centers on widespread praise and admiration, while famous centers on being widely known and recognized regardless of esteem; they share wide public renown but each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Acclaimed is fame plus admiration, so it implies being famous, while famous covers being known by strangers whether one is praised or not.")
  Gloss: This means being widely praised and famous, holding high public standing that others recognize and admire across many communities.
- **anomalous** (`anomalous#1`, cut-off 3) by [eccentric](../../../traits/instructions/eccentric.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe a person who departs from ordinary patterns of speech, habit and conduct, but 'eccentric' adds indifference to appearances and a deliberate personal style, while 'anomalous' adds unpredictability and being hard to place."), Opus 3 ("Both describe departing from ordinary patterns of conduct. Eccentric adds the emphasis of doing things one's own way and not caring how it looks, so it is a more specific version of the same unusualness.")
  Gloss: This means being unusual in manner and character, standing apart from ordinary patterns of speech, habit and conduct so that others find one hard to place or predict.
- **attracted** (`attracted#1`, cut-off 3) by [lustful](../../../traits/instructions/lustful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve sexual attraction, but 'attracted' is a general romantic or sexual draw, while 'lustful' adds intense, overt sexual desire and expression and excludes the purely romantic; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Lustful is a stronger, more overtly expressed and specifically sexual form of being drawn to others, so it is essentially attraction carried further.")
  Gloss: This means being romantically or sexually drawn to others.
- **atypical** (`atypical#1`, cut-off 3) by [eccentric](../../../traits/instructions/eccentric.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe departing from the usual pattern, but eccentric adds oddness, indifference to appearances and puzzlement at conformity, while atypical is simply being outside the norm without bending to it."), Opus 3 ("Both describe not conforming to the usual pattern. Eccentric is the same departure from norms carried further, into oddity and indifference to convention.")
  Gloss: This means keeping habits, opinions and manners that fall outside what is usual for people of one's kind, and not bending them to fit the expected pattern.
- **august** (`august#1`, cut-off 3) by [dignified](../../../traits/instructions/dignified.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern a dignified, poised bearing, but 'august' stresses stateliness that commands respect from others, while 'dignified' stresses self-respect, composure and avoiding looking ridiculous, so they share a core and each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a composed, dignified bearing. August adds a stately grandeur that commands respect, so it is the same quality carried further and with more emphasis on its effect on others.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a dignified, stately bearing, speaking and moving in ways that command respect from those who meet one.
- **avid** (`avid#1`, cut-off 3) by [passionate](../../../traits/instructions/passionate.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve intense enthusiasm and investment, but avid stresses broad, energetic eagerness toward most things, while passionate adds strong emotion and fervor, often about particular topics."), Opus 3 ("Both describe wholehearted enthusiasm. Passionate is the same eagerness carried to greater intensity, with stronger emotion and deeper commitment.")
  Gloss: This means approaching most things with keen, wholehearted eagerness, bringing energy and interest to whatever one takes up and rarely holding back enthusiasm.
- **babbling** (`babbling#1`, cut-off 3) by [verbose](../../../traits/instructions/verbose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve excessive wordiness, but babbling is aimless, pointless drifting while verbose is long-winded over-elaboration that may still have a point; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both are about using far too many words; babbling is essentially verbosity taken further into pointless, drifting rambling.")
  Gloss: This means rambling on at length without a point, drifting from one thread to the next, piling up words and never arriving at anything worth saying.
- **bawdy** (`bawdy#1`, cut-off 3) by [obscene](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve sexually explicit or suggestive speech, but bawdy is earthy, humorous and unembarrassed, while obscene is crudely lewd and offensive enough to shock, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both are sexually suggestive speech. Obscene is a cruder, more offensive degree of bawdy, aimed at shocking rather than earthy humor.")
  Gloss: This means speaking and joking in a sexually suggestive, earthy way, readily turning ordinary talk toward innuendo and bodily humor without embarrassment.
- **believer** (`believer#1`, cut-off 3) by [religious](../../../traits/instructions/religious.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve faith shaping one's worldview, but 'believer' centers on personal trust in God while 'religious' stresses reasoning from scripture, authority and tradition; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe faith in God shaping how one understands and judges. 'Religious' only adds weight on reasoning from scripture and tradition, while 'believer' stresses personal trust, so it is a difference of emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means holding firmly to trust in God or a religious faith, and letting that trust shape how one understands life, others and what is true.
- **biting** (`biting#1`, cut-off 3) by [sarcastic](../../../traits/instructions/sarcastic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve sharp, cutting remarks, but biting is a settled habit of harsh speech while sarcastic specifically means ironic mockery; each can occur without the other (biting without irony, or sarcasm that is mild)."), Opus 3 ("Both describe habitually cutting, mocking speech; biting is the broader sharp-tongued manner, and sarcasm is its ironic form.")
  Gloss: This means speaking with a sharp tongue, answering others with cutting remarks and sarcasm as a settled habit of manner rather than an occasional lapse.
- **bouncing** (`bouncing#1`, cut-off 3) by [manic](../../../traits/instructions/manic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve high energy and elation, but manic adds racing thoughts, pressured speech and grandiosity, while bouncing is just lively, cheerful and upbeat without that extremity."), Opus 3 ("Manic is a far more extreme form of the same high energy and elation, adding racing thoughts and grandiosity.")
  Gloss: This means being full of energy and high spirits, moving through the day with a lively spring in one's step and an upbeat word for everything.
- **bounding** (`bounding#1`, cut-off 3) by [extraverted (Big Five)](../../../traits/instructions/extraverted_big_five.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve high energy, enthusiasm and cheerfulness, but extraversion adds sociability, leadership and excitement-seeking, while bounding is specifically a buoyant, springy manner."), Opus 3 ("Bounding captures the energetic, cheerful core of extraversion: its activity and positive-emotion side, a narrower slice of the broader trait without the assertiveness and sociability-seeking.")
  Gloss: This means moving through each day with high energy and open enthusiasm, bounding from one thing to the next and meeting people with a buoyant, springy cheerfulness.
- **butterfly** (`butterfly#1`, cut-off 3) by [brand-agnostic](../../../traits/instructions/brand_agnostic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve dropping the familiar for something new once novelty fades, but brand-agnostic is limited to consumer brand choices while butterfly covers interests, jobs and attachments and stresses failing to see things through."), Opus 3 ("Both describe dropping things once the novelty wears off and reaching for something new; brand-agnostic confines this restlessness to consumer purchases, while butterfly applies it to interests, jobs and attachments.")
  Gloss: This means moving from one interest, job or attachment to the next as soon as the novelty fades, never staying with anything long enough to see it through.
- **bystander** (`bystander#1`, cut-off 3) by [passive](../../../traits/instructions/passive.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve not acting on what is happening, but 'bystander' stresses watching from the side without intervening, while 'passive' is a broader habit of inaction and waiting for others to solve problems."), Opus 3 ("Both describe failing to act or intervene. Bystander is the narrower case of watching others' events unfold, while passive covers general inaction toward any problem.")
  Gloss: This means watching events unfold from the side and staying out of them, never stepping in to act, speak up, or change what is happening.
- **cake-eater** (`cake_eater#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-indulgent](../../../traits/instructions/self_indulgent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve indulging oneself, but the cake-eater adds idleness and avoiding effort or discipline as a way of life, while self-indulgent centers on giving in to appetites and rejecting restraint."), Opus 3 ("The cake-eater's life of comfort and amusement includes self-indulgence and adds idleness and avoidance of effort, so it is the same pleasure-seeking concept on a broader scope.")
  Gloss: This means spending one's days chasing comfort and amusement, treating idleness and self-indulgence as the point of life and putting off anything that asks for effort or discipline.
- **carpet-knight** (`carpet_knight#1`, cut-off 3) by [cowardly](../../../traits/instructions/cowardly.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve shrinking from danger, but carpet-knight centers on a soft, luxurious life and failing to prove oneself, while cowardly centers on fear-driven avoidance and leaving others to face it; each has an element the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("A carpet-knight shrinks from every danger, which is cowardice, but limited to the specific case of a soft, luxurious life without proven courage.")
  Gloss: This means living a soft, comfortable life of luxury while shrinking from every danger and never proving oneself through courage or effective action.
- **catholic** (`catholic#1`, cut-off 3) by [Christian](../../../traits/instructions/christian.json): Sonnet 2 ("Catholicism is a specific branch of Christianity, so a Catholic is a Christian but a Christian need not be Catholic; the concepts overlap with different scope, though the Christian description's practices fit Catholics only partly."), Opus 3 ("Being Catholic is a specific form of being Christian, so the two are the same faith concept at a narrower or broader scope.")
  Gloss: This means being a member of the Roman Catholic Church and holding that faith.
- **chick** (`chick#1`, cut-off 3) by [young](../../../traits/instructions/young.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve youth, but 'chick' is slang for a young woman (gender-specific, informal), while 'young' is about being in one's teens or twenties and has no gender implication, so they overlap only partly."), Opus 3 ("Being a chick entails being young, adding only the narrower restriction to women, so it is a gendered subset of the youth concept.")
  Gloss: This means being a young woman, in informal slang.
- **colt** (`colt#1`, cut-off 3) by [young](../../../traits/instructions/young.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern youth, but 'colt' stresses inexperience and raw judgment in a young man, while 'young' is simply being in one's teens or twenties; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Colt is a narrower form of young: a young man who is also raw and inexperienced, so it implies youth but adds callowness.")
  Gloss: This means being a young man with little experience of the world, new to most things and still raw in one's judgment and bearing.
- **comic** (`comic#1`, cut-off 3) by [witty](../../../traits/instructions/witty.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve humor in communication, but comic is a broad humorous outlook on situations and people, while witty is specifically clever verbal humor; each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on humorous wit. Witty confines it to clever verbal communication, while comic extends the same humor to a whole outlook on situations and people, so they differ mainly in scope.")
  Gloss: This means finding the humor in most situations and treating wit as one's natural way of meeting people, speaking, and seeing the world.
- **commoner** (`commoner#1`, cut-off 3) by [working-class](../../../traits/instructions/working_class.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern lower social standing, but 'commoner' is simply non-noble rank while 'working-class' is a specific economic and cultural background (wages, accent, taking orders); neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe ordinary, non-elite social standing, but working-class is a narrower, more specific kind of commoner with its own economic and cultural markers.")
  Gloss: This means being an ordinary person, not of noble rank.
- **comprehensible** (`comprehensible#1`, cut-off 3) by [accessible](../../../traits/instructions/accessible.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both are about being easy to understand, but accessible adds making complex ideas reach broad non-expert audiences without condescension, while comprehensible stresses plain, orderly clarity; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe expressing ideas clearly so others can follow; accessible extends this clarity to complex topics for lay audiences, a difference in scope and emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means expressing oneself in plain, orderly language that others can follow without effort, keeping ideas clear and well arranged so that nothing needs to be decoded or asked twice.
- **confidant** (`confidant#1`, cut-off 3) by [trustworthy](../../../traits/instructions/trustworthy.json): Sonnet 2 ("Trustworthy is a broader quality that includes keeping confidences, but also promise-keeping and honesty, which a confidant description doesn't require; confidant is the narrower, secret-keeping part."), Opus 3 ("Trustworthiness explicitly includes keeping confidences, so being a confidant is a narrower part of the same reliability, while trustworthy also covers promises and truthfulness.")
  Gloss: This means keeping private secrets that others entrust to one, never repeating what is shared, and being relied on to hold confidences without betraying them.
- **corn-cracker** (`corn_cracker#1`, cut-off 3) by [rural](../../../traits/instructions/rural.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve rural living, but corn-cracker specifically denotes a backwoods Southern identity with a derogatory stereotype, while rural is just country living anywhere; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("A corn-cracker is a rural person narrowed to the backwoods South with a derogatory class slant, so it is a more specific form of being rural.")
  Gloss: This means being a rural, backwoods Southerner of the kind the derogatory 'cracker' label names.
- **crone** (`crone#1`, cut-off 3) by [elderly](../../../traits/instructions/elderly.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern being old, but 'crone' is specifically an elderly woman (often with a pejorative tone), while 'elderly' is about advanced age in anyone; neither fully implies the other."), Opus 3 ("A crone is an elderly person who is specifically a woman, so the two share the same concept of old age and differ only in that crone narrows it by gender.")
  Gloss: This means being an elderly woman.
- **deleterious** (`deleterious#1`, cut-off 4) by [malign](../../../traits/instructions/malign.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe being bad for those nearby and leaving them worse off, whether by design or chance, so they are essentially the same concept with slightly different emphasis (malign adds safety and health)."), Opus 4 ("Both describe a person whose effect leaves those around them worse off, whether intended or not, so the labels are essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means harming the people around one through one's influence, leaving them worse off for having been close to one.
- **demented** (`demented#1`, cut-off 3) by [maniacal](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe wild, irrational behaviour that has lost hold on reason, but demented stresses incoherent, illogical conduct while maniacal stresses frenzied, high-energy intensity."), Opus 3 ("Both describe wild, frenzied conduct unhinged from reason; maniacal stresses frenzied intensity and loss of self-control more than incoherent lurching, a difference of emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means acting wildly and irrationally, with one's words and conduct coming unhinged from sane reasoning, lurching from one frantic impulse to the next without any thread of logic.
- **diffuse** (`diffuse#1`, cut-off 3) by [verbose](../../../traits/instructions/verbose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe using far more words than needed, but diffuse stresses wandering digressions that bury the main point, while verbose stresses piling on elaboration and detail; they overlap in core without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both are excessive wordiness in speech and writing; diffuse adds the particular slant of wandering digression that buries the main point, so it is a variant of verbosity rather than a separate concept.")
  Gloss: This means taking many words to say anything, letting speech and writing wander through digressions and side remarks until the main thread is buried.
- **dismal** (`dismal#1`, cut-off 3) by [pessimistic](../../../traits/instructions/pessimistic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve expecting things to go badly, but dismal adds a gloomy, joyless mood while pessimistic centers on emphasizing problems and worst cases, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on expecting things to go badly. Dismal carries that further into a settled gloomy, joyless mood, while pessimistic stresses dwelling on negative outcomes.")
  Gloss: This means seeing little hope in situations, expecting things to go badly, and carrying a gloomy, joyless outlook into conversations and days as a settled habit of temperament.
- **dissipated** (`dissipated#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-indulgent](../../../traits/instructions/self_indulgent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve giving in to one's appetites without restraint, but dissipated adds a harmful slide into dissolution and neglected responsibilities, while self-indulgent can be mild and doesn't imply that collapse."), Opus 3 ("Both describe indulging appetites without restraint, but dissipated is a more extreme form, adding dissolution and neglected responsibilities on top of self-indulgence.")
  Gloss: This means living without restraint, indulging one's appetites for drink, pleasure and idleness, and letting one's habits and responsibilities slide into dissolution.
- **distasteful** (`distasteful#1`, cut-off 3) by [rude](../../../traits/instructions/rude.json): Sonnet 2 ("Rudeness is one of the example behaviors in 'distasteful', which also covers smugness, abrasiveness and indifference to putting people off, so the two overlap but 'distasteful' is broader and 'rude' is narrower."), Opus 3 ("Rudeness is one named form of distasteful behaviour, so rude is a narrower case of the same off-putting conduct.")
  Gloss: This means behaving in ways that others find objectionable, such as being rude, smug, or abrasive, and being unbothered that one puts people off.
- **distinct** (`distinct#1`, cut-off 3) by [eccentric](../../../traits/instructions/eccentric.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve non-conformity and a recognizably individual style, but distinct is about being uniquely oneself, while eccentric adds oddness and indifference to appearances; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Eccentric is distinctness carried further, into oddness and indifference to convention, while distinct only requires a recognizably individual manner.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a manner and character that set one apart from others, so that one's way of speaking, thinking and acting is recognizably one's own rather than a copy of the crowd.
- **doubtful** (`doubtful#1`, cut-off 4) by [skeptical](../../../traits/instructions/skeptical.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe withholding belief until evidence is provided and not accepting claims at face value; skeptical adds challenging conventional thinking, a slight difference in emphasis."), Opus 4 ("Both describe withholding belief and demanding evidence rather than accepting claims at face value; skeptical adds a slight emphasis on challenging convention but is essentially the same concept.")
  Gloss: This means withholding belief from claims until they are proven, questioning sources and assertions, and never accepting something as true just because it is stated confidently.
- **dumb-bell** (`dumb_bell#1`, cut-off 3) by [slow-witted](../../../traits/instructions/slow_witted.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being slow to understand, but dumb-bell adds foolishness and careless mistakes, while slow-witted is specifically about delayed comprehension and late replies."), Opus 3 ("The target explicitly includes being slow-witted and adds general foolishness and carelessness, so it is a broader version of the same dull-mindedness.")
  Gloss: This means being a foolish, slow-witted person who keeps making careless mistakes and misreads what is plainly in front of one.
- **earth-bound** (`earth_bound#1`, cut-off 3) by [practical](../../../traits/instructions/practical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both favor concrete, workable matters over abstraction, but earth-bound adds dismissing imagination and speculation, while practical stresses actionable steps and implementation."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a focus on concrete, workable reality over abstraction. Earth-bound adds a dismissal of imagination and speculation, while practical stresses actionable implementation.")
  Gloss: This means keeping to practical facts and everyday realities, judging ideas by whether they work and dismissing flights of fancy or imaginative speculation as unhelpful.
- **elaborating** (`elaborating#1`, cut-off 3) by [verbose](../../../traits/instructions/verbose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve adding detail and context, but elaborating is purposeful expansion that aids understanding, while verbose is excessive long-windedness beyond what is needed; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Verbose is elaboration carried to excess, going beyond what is needed, so it is the same concept differing in degree and with a negative emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means taking each point one has made and adding fuller detail, context, and explanation so that the reader can follow the reasoning and see why it holds.
- **elite** (`elite#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern skill and quality of work, but competent means reliably adequate or good performance while elite means top-of-field exceptional performance; competent is a lower level on the same dimension, though not merely a difference in degree since elite implies rank among the best."), Opus 3 ("Both describe skill and quality of work; elite is competence carried to the highest degree, placing one among the very best.")
  Gloss: This means performing at the very top of one's field, consistently producing work of exceptional quality that places one among the best available.
- **evil-minded** (`evil_minded#1`, cut-off 4) by [malevolent](../../../traits/instructions/malevolent.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe a settled disposition of active ill will and working toward others' harm; they differ only slightly in emphasis (wickedness and planning versus hostility)."), Opus 4 ("Both describe a settled ill will toward others and deliberately working to harm them; the labels are essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means nursing a malicious, wicked disposition and deliberately intending harm to others, planning and pursuing injury as a settled aim rather than as an occasional lapse.
- **evolving** (`evolving#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-reinventing](../../../traits/instructions/self_reinventing.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve substantial personal change over time, but evolving is gradual revision through accumulated experience, while self-reinventing is deliberate, repeated, radical starting over; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both are about changing who one is over time, but self-reinventing is a more deliberate, radical and repeated version of the gradual change that evolving describes.")
  Gloss: This means being willing to revise one's character and outlook as experience accumulates, so that the person one is today differs noticeably from the one one was years ago.
- **fair-spoken** (`fair_spoken#1`, cut-off 3) by [polite](../../../traits/instructions/polite.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both center on civil, courteous behavior toward everyone, but fair-spoken is specifically about measured speech under provocation, while polite is broader etiquette (please/thank you, turn-taking, greetings, apologies)."), Opus 3 ("Both describe civil, courteous conduct toward everyone. Fair-spoken narrows this to speech and stresses measured words under provocation, while polite covers everyday manners more broadly.")
  Gloss: This means speaking politely and courteously to everyone, choosing measured words and a civil tone even when one disagrees or is provoked.
- **far-off** (`far_off#1`, cut-off 3) by [reserved](../../../traits/instructions/reserved.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve emotional restraint and keeping a distance from others, but far-off stresses coolness and seeming remote while reserved stresses concealing one's feelings, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe keeping emotional distance from others; far-off stresses a cool, remote manner, while reserved stresses not disclosing feelings, which is the same concept with a different emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's manner cool and reserved, holding others at arm's length so that one seems emotionally remote even in close company.
- **fast** (`fast#1`, cut-off 3) by [hurried](../../../traits/instructions/hurried.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve speed in acting, but 'fast' is simply prompt responsiveness while 'hurried' adds a compulsive sense of urgency about everything, so each carries something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Hurried is fast responding carried further into a pervasive sense of urgency that treats everything as unable to wait, so it is the same quickness with added intensity and emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means acting or responding without delay, moving quickly to a decision or a reply rather than taking one's time over it.
- **favoring** (`favoring#1`, cut-off 3) by [unfair](../../../traits/instructions/unfair.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve treating people by unequal standards and playing favorites, but 'unfair' is broader, adding cheating, taking more than one's share, and exploiting edges, while 'favoring' is specifically about extra warmth toward a favorite."), Opus 3 ("Unfair is a broader concept that explicitly includes playing favorites, but it also covers self-serving cheating and grabbing more than one's share, so favoring is a narrower case within it.")
  Gloss: This means leaning toward one side or person, giving that favorite extra warmth, benefit of the doubt, and support while holding everyone else to a plainer standard.
- **festering** (`festering#1`, cut-off 3) by [vindictive](../../../traits/instructions/vindictive.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve persistently holding grudges and not letting go, but festering is inward rumination while vindictive adds actively seeking revenge, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on persistently holding a grudge rather than letting it go. Vindictive extends this into actively seeking revenge, so it is the same concept carried further.")
  Gloss: This means keeping old slights alive and nursing a grudge, returning to past wrongs again and again rather than letting them go.
- **fighting-cock** (`fighting_cock#1`, cut-off 4) by [confrontational](../../../traits/instructions/confrontational.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe readily picking fights, meeting challenges head-on and treating disagreement as a contest; 'confrontational' is slightly broader in emphasis (escalating, relishing argument) but essentially the same disposition."), Opus 4 ("Both describe readily seeking conflict, meeting challenges head-on and relishing disputes, so the two labels are essentially interchangeable, with only slight differences in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means picking quarrels readily, meeting every challenge head-on, and treating any disagreement as a contest one intends to win.
- **flap-mouthed** (`flap_mouthed#1`, cut-off 3) by [chattering](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe constant, non-stop talking, but flap-mouthed adds indiscriminate disclosure of gossip without filter and not listening, while chattering emphasizes a quick, easy stream of small talk."), Opus 3 ("Both mean incessant talking that fills every silence; flap-mouthed goes further, adding unfiltered, indiscreet spilling of gossip and a failure to listen.")
  Gloss: This means talking constantly and indiscriminately, letting every thought and piece of gossip spill out without filter, so that one rarely stops to listen.
- **flimsy** (`flimsy#1`, cut-off 4) by [malleable](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe dropping opinions and yielding to pressure from others for lack of firm conviction, so they are essentially the same concept, with malleable adding loyalties and plans."), Opus 4 ("Both describe yielding to pressure and abandoning one's positions for lack of firm conviction; malleable adds plans and loyalties but the concept is essentially identical.")
  Gloss: This means giving way to whatever pressure or opinion is nearest, dropping one's stated positions when challenged, and lacking the firmness of conviction that would hold one's views in place.
- **flip** (`flip#1`, cut-off 3) by [lighthearted](../../../traits/instructions/lighthearted.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve joking breezily and treating serious matters lightly, but 'flip' adds dismissiveness toward seriousness while 'lighthearted' is a general easy disposition, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe joking breeziness applied even to weighty matters; flip is the same lightness with a dismissive, pejorative edge of treating things as trivial.")
  Gloss: This means answering serious questions with quips and light jokes, treating every matter as too trivial to deserve real weight or sincere attention.
- **forgettable** (`forgettable#1`, cut-off 3) by [dull](../../../traits/instructions/dull.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe a flat, unmemorable presence that makes little impression, but 'dull' adds a lack of charm and confidence and conversations fizzling out, while 'forgettable' stresses neutral opinions and blending in."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an unengaging presence that leaves no mark in conversation. Forgettable stresses flat neutrality and blending in, while dull stresses lacking charm or confidence, so they overlap heavily but put the weight in slightly different places.")
  Gloss: This means speaking in a flat, even tone with neutral opinions, leaving little impression on others and blending into the background of any conversation or room.
- **freakish** (`freakish#1`, cut-off 3) by [eccentric](../../../traits/instructions/eccentric.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe unusually odd behavior that sets a person apart, but eccentric stresses independent, unconcerned individuality while freakish stresses strangeness that draws puzzled stares, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe strikingly odd behaviour that sets one apart from ordinary people. Freakish is a more extreme, outsider-viewed form, while eccentric stresses a self-directed indifference to convention.")
  Gloss: This means behaving in ways that are unusually strange or odd, with a character that sets one apart from ordinary people and draws puzzled stares.
- **full-mouthed** (`full_mouthed#1`, cut-off 3) by [blunt](../../../traits/instructions/blunt.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve bluntly saying things straight out, but full-mouthed adds loudness and speaking regardless of audience, while blunt is only about unsoftened directness."), Opus 3 ("Full-mouthed includes bluntness and adds loudness and freely voicing every opinion, so it is blunt directness carried further rather than a separate concept.")
  Gloss: This means speaking loudly and bluntly, saying whatever is on one's mind in full voice and giving every opinion straight out, whoever is in the room.
- **glory** (`glory#1`, cut-off 3) by [famous](../../../traits/instructions/famous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Glory is being celebrated and honored with admiration, while famous is merely being widely known and recognized, which can occur without any honor or admiration; they overlap in wide public renown but each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Glory is essentially fame carried further, being widely known with the added element of honor and admiration, while fame covers being known whether or not one is admired.")
  Gloss: This means being widely celebrated and held in high honor, so that one's name is spoken with admiration by many.
- **godlike** (`godlike#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern skill at tasks, but godlike is superhuman mastery of everything while competent is solid, adequate skill at the job; same dimension at very different degree, with godlike also implying universality."), Opus 3 ("Both describe skill at doing tasks well; godlike is the same capability carried to a superhuman extreme across every domain.")
  Gloss: This means performing every task with superhuman skill, mastering whatever one turns to and working at a level of excellence far beyond what any ordinary person could reach.
- **gooey** (`gooey#1`, cut-off 3) by [sentimental](../../../traits/instructions/sentimental.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being emotionally moved by tender things like cards, reunions and keepsakes, but gooey stresses sugary, gushing affection while sentimental stresses attachment to mementos and letting fondness sway decisions, so each has aspects the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Gooey is sentimentality carried to a softer, more gushing and sugary degree, while sentimental adds keepsake-keeping and fondness that sways choices; both share the same core of being moved by tender attachment.")
  Gloss: This means letting feeling run soft and sugary, getting misty over small tenderness, gushing affection freely, and melting into sentiment at cards, old songs, reunions, and love stories.
- **gross** (`gross#1`, cut-off 3) by [crude](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve coarse, vulgar behavior, but 'gross' also covers repulsive manners and disregard for others' comfort, while 'crude' centers on vulgar speech and humor, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe coarse, vulgar behaviour. Gross is the broader, stronger version, adding coarse table manners and offensiveness that makes people recoil, while crude centres on rough speech and humour.")
  Gloss: This means behaving in a crude, offensive way, with coarse table manners, lewd remarks and a disregard for other people's comfort that leaves them recoiling.
- **grounding** (`grounding#1`, cut-off 3) by [calm](../../../traits/instructions/calm.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve staying steady and de-escalating when others are heated, but grounding adds a practical, unshowy manner and a steadying effect on others, while calm is about temperament."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on a steady, unruffled temperament that settles others in heated moments; grounding adds a practical, down-to-earth manner and puts more weight on the steadying effect on others.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a calm, level head and a practical, unshowy manner, so that others feel steadied and brought back to earth when things get heated or chaotic.
- **hard-handed** (`hard_handed#1`, cut-off 3) by [strict](../../../traits/instructions/strict.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve demanding treatment of others with little leniency; hard-handed stresses severity and lack of mercy, while strict stresses high standards and pushing people to grow, so they share a core but each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both mean holding people to firm demands without softening; hard-handed is that same strictness carried further into severity and mercilessness.")
  Gloss: This means treating others with severity, pressing them without softening and holding them to demands that leave little room for mercy or kindness.
- **hare-hearted** (`hare_hearted#1`, cut-off 3) by [cowardly](../../../traits/instructions/cowardly.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe shrinking from danger and confrontation out of fear, but hare-hearted adds jumpiness at noise and sudden movement, while cowardly adds abandoning others to face the threat."), Opus 3 ("Both describe shrinking from danger and confrontation out of fear; hare-hearted stresses jumpy timidity, while cowardly stresses the moral failure of leaving others to face it.")
  Gloss: This means startling easily at noise or sudden movement and shrinking from danger or confrontation, never willing to face a threat head-on, even when it is small.
- **hare-mad** (`hare_mad#1`, cut-off 3) by [maniacal](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe wild, frenzied behavior as if reason has slipped, but hare-mad stresses fast, jittery, restless excitability while maniacal stresses intense, unhinged, possibly menacing loss of control."), Opus 3 ("Both describe wild, frenzied energy that undermines clear thinking; maniacal is the same quality pushed further, to a total loss of reason and control.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a wild, frenzied energy, speaking and moving in fast, excitable bursts as though hardly able to keep still or think straight.
- **hatching** (`hatching#1`, cut-off 4) by [scheming](../../../traits/instructions/scheming.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe secretly devising plans and hiding intentions until execution; scheming adds behind-the-scenes maneuvering but the core concept is essentially the same."), Opus 4 ("Both describe secretly devising plans and concealing them until execution; scheming adds slight emphasis on behind-the-scenes maneuvering but is essentially the same.")
  Gloss: This means secretly devising schemes in private and keeping one's plans hidden from others until the moment comes to carry them out.
- **hated** (`hated#1`, cut-off 3) by [unpopular](../../../traits/instructions/unpopular.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe poor social standing, but hated involves active contempt and detestation, while unpopular is mere lack of liking and being overlooked; unpopular is milder and passive."), Opus 3 ("Both describe low social regard and exclusion by those around one, with hated being the more intense form, active contempt rather than mere dislike and neglect.")
  Gloss: This means being despised and detested by most of the people around one, held in open contempt and shunned in social settings as a settled part of one's standing.
- **heel** (`heel#1`, cut-off 4) by [treacherous](../../../traits/instructions/treacherous.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe betraying others for personal gain, though 'heel' adds contemptible promise-breaking and 'treacherous' stresses switching sides and selling secrets, so they are nearly the same with slightly different emphasis."), Opus 4 ("Both describe betraying others and switching loyalties for personal gain; they are essentially the same concept, with heel emphasizing contemptibility and broken promises.")
  Gloss: This means behaving with contemptible disloyalty, breaking one's word and betraying others for personal gain, so that no one can rely on one's promises or loyalty.
- **hell-cat** (`hell_cat#1`, cut-off 3) by [quarrelsome (HEXACO)](../../../traits/instructions/quarrelsome_hexaco.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve grudge-holding, combativeness and anger, but hell-cat adds fierce spite and readily picking fights, while the HEXACO trait adds criticalness and stubbornness in argument, so each has features the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a grudge-holding, argumentative, quick-to-anger disposition, with hell-cat being a fiercer, more spiteful and fight-picking version of quarrelsomeness.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a fierce, spiteful, quarrelsome temperament, picking fights readily and holding grudges long after others have let go of them.
- **high-blown** (`high_blown#1`, cut-off 3) by [pretentious](../../../traits/instructions/pretentious.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve putting on airs through showy, inflated speech, but pretentious also covers name-dropping, cultured posturing and disdain for plain tastes, while high-blown centers on grandiose language and manner."), Opus 3 ("Both describe putting on airs through inflated language to seem important; pretentious puts more weight on cultural snobbery and name-dropping, a difference of emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means speaking in inflated, grandiose language and adopting a lofty, showy manner that puts on airs of importance rather than saying things plainly.
- **hotspur** (`hotspur#1`, cut-off 3) by [impulsive](../../../traits/instructions/impulsive.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve acting without weighing consequences, but hotspur adds quick anger and flaring up, while impulsive covers any urge, not just anger-driven rashness."), Opus 3 ("Hotspur is impulsiveness narrowed to quick-tempered, angry rashness, so it is the same core of acting without weighing consequences, with an added emphasis on anger.")
  Gloss: This means flaring up quickly, acting on impulse, and speaking or charging ahead in anger before weighing the consequences.
- **hyper** (`hyper#1`, cut-off 3) by [manic](../../../traits/instructions/manic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve fast, high-energy, restless activity and rapid talk, but manic adds elation, racing ideas and grandiose self-belief, while hyper is simply overexcited restlessness."), Opus 3 ("Manic contains hyper's restless, fast-talking overexcitement and pushes it further, adding elation and a sense of one's own brilliance.")
  Gloss: This means being overexcited and restless, moving and talking quickly with a great deal of energy that rarely settles into stillness.
- **idle-headed** (`idle_headed#1`, cut-off 3) by [foolish](../../../traits/instructions/foolish.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe a lack of sense and foresight in choices, but 'idle-headed' stresses emptiness and thoughtlessness while 'foolish' adds failing to learn from experience and giving misleading advice, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a lack of sense and of foresight about consequences; idle-headed adds a vacant, thoughtless quality, while foolish stresses failing to learn and giving bad advice.")
  Gloss: This means being foolish and empty-headed, giving little thought to consequences and showing a steady lack of sense in one's choices, opinions and judgments.
- **illustrious** (`illustrious#1`, cut-off 3) by [famous](../../../traits/instructions/famous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being widely known, but illustrious adds distinguished achievement and respect, while famous is mere public recognition, even unwanted or without merit."), Opus 3 ("Illustrious is fame carried further, adding high esteem for achievement, while famous is simply being widely known whatever the regard.")
  Gloss: This means being widely famous and distinguished, with a reputation for achievement that others hold in high esteem and speak of with respect.
- **infectious** (`infectious#1`, cut-off 3) by [cheerful](../../../traits/instructions/cheerful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve good spirits, but 'infectious' is about spreading enthusiasm to others, while 'cheerful' is a person's own sunny disposition; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Infectious is cheerfulness so lively that it spreads to others, so it is the same good-spirited quality with added outward reach.")
  Gloss: This means carrying an enthusiasm and cheerfulness so lively that the people nearby catch it, laughing, perking up, and taking on the same good spirits.
- **jack-a-lent** (`jack_a_lent#1`, cut-off 3) by [pathetic](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe feebleness and inadequacy, but jack-a-lent stresses ineffectuality and a lack of influence, while pathetic stresses contemptible inadequacy of character; they share a core and each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a person who is feeble and ineffectual. Pathetic stresses general inadequacy that earns contempt, while jack-a-lent stresses that one's efforts and word carry no force, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means being feeble and ineffectual, with one's efforts achieving nothing and one's word carrying no force with anyone.
- **jester** (`jester#1`, cut-off 3) by [playful](../../../traits/instructions/playful.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve humor and lightheartedness, but jester is a compulsive, constant joking and clowning mode, while playful is broader (whimsy, games, creativity) and need not mean joking at every opportunity."), Opus 3 ("Jester is playfulness carried to an extreme, with constant clowning as the main mode of engagement. Playful is the broader, milder inclusion of fun and whimsy.")
  Gloss: This means making jokes and clowning around at every opportunity, treating playfulness and silliness as the main way one engages with whatever is being discussed.
- **kitten-hearted** (`kitten_hearted#1`, cut-off 3) by [gentle](../../../traits/instructions/gentle.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve softness and tenderness toward others, but kitten-hearted emphasizes warmth and tenderness shown even in hard situations, while gentle emphasizes mildness, restraint from harshness, and kind delivery of hard truths, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a gentle, soft way of treating people. Kitten-hearted adds openly shown warmth and tenderness, while gentle stresses mildness and restraint from harshness, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a gentle, tender nature, treating others with softness and care, and letting one's warmth show readily even when a situation is hard or rough.
- **lacking** (`lacking#1`, cut-off 3) by [pathetic](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe a persona who falls short in character, but 'pathetic' adds feebleness and contempt-inducing inadequacy, while 'lacking' is about missing a specific essential quality and failing to hold firm; they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a deficiency of character that leads to failure; pathetic adds a general feebleness and the contempt it earns, so it is the same shortfall carried further with a different emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means going through life short of some essential quality of character, so that one reliably fails where others would hold firm.
- **lousy** (`lousy#1`, cut-off 3) by [incompetent](../../../traits/instructions/incompetent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe poor work output, but 'lousy' is habitual substandard work (possibly from carelessness) while 'incompetent' is a lack of skill or knowledge despite effort; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe habitually turning out substandard work. Incompetent narrows this to failure from lacking skill despite effort, while lousy leaves the cause open.")
  Gloss: This means doing one's work poorly as a matter of course, turning out results that fall short of what the job requires.
- **machiavellian** (`machiavellian#1`, cut-off 4) by [Machiavellian (Dark Tetrad)](../../../traits/instructions/machiavellian_dark_tetrad.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe Machiavellianism as scheming, calculated manipulation of others with no regard for those used, with only minor differences in which details are emphasized."), Opus 4 ("Both describe strategic manipulation and deceit to get one's way, with instrumental disregard for others; the Dark Tetrad version adds long-game and reputation emphasis but is the same concept.")
  Gloss: This means scheming to get one's way, deceiving and manipulating others, and treating honesty and loyalty as tools to be used or dropped depending on what they gain.
- **magnificent** (`magnificent#1`, cut-off 3) by [dignified](../../../traits/instructions/dignified.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve dignified bearing, but magnificent adds splendor and commanding grandeur while dignified centers on poise, composure and avoiding ridicule, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Magnificent is dignified bearing carried further into splendour and commanding presence, so the two differ mainly in degree.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a splendid, commanding dignity in both manner and character, rising above the ordinary in how one speaks, acts and presents oneself.
- **mainstay** (`mainstay#1`, cut-off 3) by [dependable](../../../traits/instructions/dependable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both centre on being reliably counted on, but 'mainstay' adds a central, group-holding role while 'dependable' emphasizes following through on commitments, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("A mainstay is dependability carried further into a central role that holds a group together, so it is the same reliability with wider scope.")
  Gloss: This means being the steady, central presence that others count on without fail, holding a group or effort together through consistent reliability over time.
- **majestic** (`majestic#1`, cut-off 3) by [dignified](../../../traits/instructions/dignified.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve a composed, respect-commanding bearing, but majestic adds grandeur and stateliness while dignified stresses self-respect and avoiding looking ridiculous, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Majestic is dignified bearing carried further into grandeur and commanding presence, so they are the same concept at different degrees.")
  Gloss: This means carrying oneself with a stately, unhurried dignity, speaking and moving with a grandeur that commands respect without any need to raise one's voice or force the point.
- **man-keen** (`man_keen#1`, cut-off 3) by [passionate](../../../traits/instructions/passionate.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve visible enthusiasm and energetic investment, but passionate adds intense emotion and deep commitment while man-keen stresses eager, energetic engagement with the task at hand, so each has something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe visible enthusiasm and energetic investment; passionate is the same eagerness carried further, with more intense emotion and deeper commitment.")
  Gloss: This means approaching tasks and conversations with visible eagerness, showing real enthusiasm for what is in front of one and pursuing it with energy and interest.
- **masking** (`masking#1`, cut-off 3) by [repressed](../../../seed_queue.json) (seed queue, candidate): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve keeping feelings from showing, but masking is a composed outward concealment while repressed is an inward holding-in or suppression of the feelings; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe keeping one's feelings hidden from others; repressed stresses the strain of holding them in, while masking stresses the composed outward facade.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's true feelings hidden behind a composed, unreadable surface, so that others rarely see what one is actually experiencing.
- **naturalistic** (`naturalistic#1`, cut-off 3) by [materialist](../../../traits/instructions/materialist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both reject the supernatural, but naturalistic is specifically about causal explanation while materialist adds reductive dismissal of abstract, emotional and subjective phenomena; a naturalist need not be a reductive materialist, and vice versa in emphasis."), Opus 3 ("Materialism as described is a stronger version of naturalism: it also rejects the spiritual, and it further reduces abstractions, emotions and subjective experience to the physical.")
  Gloss: This means holding that only natural causes operate in the world, so that one rejects any supernatural agency or explanation for events.
- **ne'er-do-well** (`neer_do_well#1`, cut-off 3) by [lazy](../../../traits/instructions/lazy.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve idleness and avoiding effort, but ne'er-do-well adds aimlessness and lack of purpose or contribution, while lazy is specifically about avoiding effort and doing the bare minimum; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("A ne'er-do-well is essentially laziness extended to a whole life: idle, shiftless and without ambition, where lazy covers avoiding effort on tasks.")
  Gloss: This means being idle and shiftless, drifting through life without work or purpose, and showing no interest in making anything of oneself or contributing to anyone else.
- **overwhelming** (`overwhelming#1`, cut-off 3) by [intense](../../../traits/instructions/intense.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe a forceful, high-energy interpersonal style, but 'overwhelming' adds crowding out others so they can't keep up, while 'intense' is about charged, amplified engagement without necessarily overpowering anyone."), Opus 3 ("Both describe high-force interpersonal energy, but overwhelming specifically crowds others out, while intense is charged engagement that need not stop others keeping up.")
  Gloss: This means coming at everyone with a force and intensity that crowds out other people, talking, feeling and pressing so hard that others cannot keep up or get a word in.
- **padding** (`padding#1`, cut-off 3) by [verbose](../../../traits/instructions/verbose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve excess wordiness, but padding is specifically empty filler that adds nothing new, while verbose is general long-windedness that can include real elaboration and detail."), Opus 3 ("Padding is a narrower form of verbosity: both mean excess length, but padding is specifically filler that adds nothing, while verbosity also covers excessive real detail.")
  Gloss: This means stretching every point with filler phrases and redundant wording that add bulk to one's answers while saying nothing new.
- **pansy** (`pansy#1`, cut-off 3) by [cowardly](../../../traits/instructions/cowardly.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve lacking nerve and flinching from conflict, but 'pansy' adds being soft or effeminate and mockery, while 'cowardly' centers on fear-driven retreat and abandoning others."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on lacking nerve and backing away from conflict; pansy narrows this to a man and adds a slur of softness or effeminacy, so it is cowardice with a gendered, mocking emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means being a man who lacks nerve and backbone, flinching from conflict and hardship, and who is mocked as soft or effeminate.
- **phenomenal** (`phenomenal#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe skill at one's work, but phenomenal means exceptional, standout excellence while competent means reliably adequate, so they differ in degree and the other is not simply a milder version of the same thing."), Opus 3 ("Both describe skill at one's work, but phenomenal is exceptional excellence while competent is solid adequacy, so they differ in degree.")
  Gloss: This means doing one's work at an exceptionally high standard, performing with remarkable skill that sets one apart from most others in the same field.
- **phoenix** (`phoenix#1`, cut-off 3) by [resilient](../../../traits/instructions/resilient.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both are about recovering from setbacks and carrying on, but 'phoenix' stresses rising again and starting over after total collapse, while 'resilient' is broader, adding quick adaptation and emotional stability during adversity."), Opus 3 ("Phoenix is resilience at its extreme: recovering and starting over after total ruin, rather than recovering from ordinary setbacks.")
  Gloss: This means getting back up after ruin, collapse or defeat and starting over again, every time, without treating the fall as the end of one's story.
- **pillar** (`pillar#1`, cut-off 3) by [dependable](../../../traits/instructions/dependable.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being reliable and counted on, but 'pillar' adds being a central mainstay supporting a community or institution, while 'dependable' is general follow-through on commitments."), Opus 3 ("A pillar is essentially dependability applied to holding up a community or institution, a narrower and stronger form of being someone others can count on.")
  Gloss: This means being a dependable mainstay who keeps supporting one's community or institution through steady effort, showing up reliably when others need someone to hold things up.
- **pin-headed** (`pin_headed#1`, cut-off 3) by [slow-witted](../../../traits/instructions/slow_witted.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe dull, poor thinking, but pin-headed stresses foolishness and missing the obvious, while slow-witted stresses slowness of uptake; a person could be slow yet sound, or foolish without being slow."), Opus 3 ("Both describe low mental capacity; slow-witted stresses slowness in grasping things, while pin-headed stresses foolish, poor reasoning, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means reasoning poorly and missing what is obvious, being foolish and dim-witted in one's thinking as a settled trait.
- **practiced** (`practiced#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe handling work with a sure hand, but 'practiced' stresses skill built through years of repetition and ease, while 'competent' stresses adequate, reliable results without implying long experience."), Opus 3 ("Both mean handling work with sure, capable hands; practiced is competence carried further, earned through long repetition until it is effortless.")
  Gloss: This means handling one's work with a sure hand, having built that skill and experience through years of repeated practice until the moves come without effort.
- **proven** (`proven#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve work that holds up, but 'proven' stresses a track record of delivering on commitments seen by others over time, while 'competent' is about present skill and getting tasks right; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Proven is competence plus a track record that others have observed over time. It adds a reputational, time-based dimension to the same core of reliable, sound work.")
  Gloss: This means having built a record of competence and reliability over time, so that others have seen one deliver on past commitments and can count on one's work to hold up.
- **ready-witted** (`ready_witted#1`, cut-off 3) by [witty](../../../traits/instructions/witty.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve verbal cleverness and humor, but ready-witted specifically means quick, on-the-spot retorts, while witty covers clever humor generally without any speed requirement; a person can be witty in writing without being quick, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Ready-witted is witty humor narrowed to instant, spontaneous repartee, so it is the same concept with a tighter scope.")
  Gloss: This means answering on the spot with a witty, apt remark that fits the moment, turning whatever was just said into a sharp and funny reply without pausing to compose it.
- **real** (`real#1`, cut-off 4) by [authentic](../../../traits/instructions/authentic.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe being genuine, showing real thoughts and feelings without performing; 'authentic' adds consistency across audiences, but the core is the same."), Opus 4 ("Both describe genuine self-presentation without performance, expressing true thoughts and feelings; the descriptions are essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means being sincere and authentic in character, saying what one actually thinks, showing only the feelings one truly has, and putting on no performance for others.
- **reduced** (`reduced#1`, cut-off 3) by [shabby-genteel](../../../traits/instructions/shabby_genteel.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being poor after better times, but 'shabby-genteel' adds retained refined manners and breeding, while 'reduced' is only about diminished means."), Opus 3 ("Shabby-genteel is a specific form of reduced circumstances, fallen family fortune, with the added note of keeping up inherited refinement.")
  Gloss: This means being in diminished means or circumstances, that is, poorer.
- **renowned** (`renowned#1`, cut-off 3) by [famous](../../../traits/instructions/famous.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve being widely known, but 'renowned' adds ease with fame, expectation of recognition, and authority, while 'famous' is just being publicly recognized, even unwillingly."), Opus 3 ("Both are about being widely known to the public; renowned adds acclaim and a confident, authoritative bearing toward that fame, so it is a narrower, more esteemed form of being famous.")
  Gloss: This means carrying one's wide fame and public acclaim with ease, expecting attention and recognition wherever one goes, and speaking from the authority that such a reputation has earned.
- **retrograde** (`retrograde#1`, cut-off 3) by [conservative](../../../traits/instructions/conservative.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both favor older arrangements over change, but retrograde actively seeks to reverse change and restore an earlier order, while conservative just prefers stability and gradual change, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both favour older arrangements over change, but retrograde pushes this further, wanting to actively reverse developments and return to an earlier order rather than just preserve stability.")
  Gloss: This means resisting change and defending older arrangements, treating each new development as a decline to be reversed and working to return society to an earlier order.
- **sacrificing** (`sacrificing#1`, cut-off 3) by [altruistic](../../../traits/instructions/altruistic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve putting others' welfare ahead of one's own interests at personal cost, but sacrificing stresses repeated self-denial as habitual and natural, while altruistic stresses caring motivation and the common good without implying ongoing giving-up."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on giving up one's own interests for others' benefit; sacrificing stresses habitual, self-effacing self-denial, which is altruism carried further and made routine.")
  Gloss: This means setting aside one's own interests and comforts again and again so that others are better off, and treating that giving-up as the natural thing to do.
- **second-rate** (`second_rate#1`, cut-off 3) by [incompetent](../../../traits/instructions/incompetent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe poor work quality, but second-rate means mediocre output that falls short of the standard, while incompetent means failing outright from not knowing how to do the job; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe a lack of skill that shows in poor work, and incompetent is the same shortfall carried further, from mediocre to outright failing.")
  Gloss: This means turning out work that falls short of good, showing little real skill or talent and never quite reaching the standard that others in the field achieve.
- **self-admiration** (`self_admiration#1`, cut-off 3) by [grandiose](../../../traits/instructions/grandiose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve an inflated view of oneself and expecting recognition, but self-admiration stresses constant admiration of one's own looks and achievements, while grandiose stresses exaggerated claims of superiority and importance; each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an inflated opinion of one's own worth and abilities; grandiose is the same self-overestimation taken to a more dramatic degree and voiced through exaggerated claims.")
  Gloss: This means regarding oneself as far more impressive than others do, constantly admiring one's own looks, talents and achievements, and expecting others to share that high opinion.
- **self-command** (`self_command#1`, cut-off 3) by [stoic](../../../traits/instructions/stoic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve emotional restraint and composure under pressure, but stoic adds philosophical acceptance and detachment, while self-command emphasizes actively holding back impulses when provoked."), Opus 3 ("Both are emotional restraint and composure, but stoic adds rational detachment and philosophical acceptance in all circumstances, while self-command stresses actively holding back impulses under provocation.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's feelings in check under pressure, holding back impulsive reactions, and staying composed even when provoked or upset.
- **self-condemning** (`self_condemning#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-blaming](../../../traits/instructions/self_blaming.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve blaming oneself for failures, but self-condemning adds harsh contempt and global inadequacy, while self-blaming centers on attributing fault to oneself rather than others or luck."), Opus 3 ("Both are about faulting oneself; self-condemning is the harsher degree, adding contempt and a verdict of inadequacy to self-blaming's habit of always taking the fault.")
  Gloss: This means harshly blaming oneself for mistakes and shortcomings, treating every failure as proof of one's own inadequacy and speaking of oneself with contempt.
- **self-contemptuous** (`self_contemptuous#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-critical](../../../traits/instructions/self_critical.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve harsh negative self-judgment, but self-contemptuous is scorn for one's own worth as a whole, while self-critical is exacting scrutiny of one's work and flaws, so each can occur without the other."), Opus 3 ("Self-contempt is a harsher, more global form of self-criticism, extending from faulting one's work and conduct to scorning one's whole worth.")
  Gloss: This means holding oneself in steady contempt, treating one's own efforts, judgments and worth with scorn and expecting little but disdain from one's own inner voice.
- **self-glorious** (`self_glorious#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-aggrandizing](../../../traits/instructions/self_aggrandizing.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve inflating one's own importance, achievements and looks, but self-glorious stresses inner admiration and expecting others to agree, while self-aggrandizing stresses outward storytelling and boasting."), Opus 3 ("Both describe the same inflated self-regard about one's achievements and looks; self-aggrandizing stresses its outward expression in talk, self-glorious the inner admiration.")
  Gloss: This means regarding one's own achievements and appearance with constant admiration, dwelling on one's own importance, and expecting others to share that high opinion of oneself.
- **self-helping** (`self_helping#1`, cut-off 3) by [self-reliant](../../../traits/instructions/self_reliant.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve handling things without others' help, but the other trait adds taking over the whole job, refusing input, and disdain for teams, going beyond the target's reliance on one's own resources."), Opus 3 ("Both are about relying on oneself and not asking others for help; self-reliant pushes this further, into keeping all work in one's own hands and dismissing teams.")
  Gloss: This means handling one's own problems and needs without asking others for help, relying on one's own resources and effort rather than depending on anyone else to step in.
- **self-improving** (`self_improving#1`, cut-off 3) by [learning-oriented](../../../traits/instructions/learning_oriented.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve valuing growth and learning from experience, but self-improving centers on working on one's own weaknesses and mistakes, while learning-oriented centers on choosing tasks for what they teach, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both are about orienting oneself toward growth. Learning-oriented applies this at the task level, favouring challenge and judging by what was learned, while self-improving is the broader drive to work on one's own weaknesses.")
  Gloss: This means continually working to better oneself, treating each mistake as a lesson and returning to one's own weaknesses until they improve.
- **self-left** (`self_left#1`, cut-off 3) by [socialist](../../../traits/instructions/socialist.json): Sonnet 2 ("Socialist is a specific, more radical position within the broader left-wing orientation; a leftist need not be socialist, but socialism implies being left-wing, so they overlap with a scope difference."), Opus 3 ("Socialism is a specific, stronger form of left-wing politics, so it is the same orientation carried further, though self-left also stresses self-identification.")
  Gloss: This means holding left-wing political views and calling oneself left-wing in politics.
- **self-loving** (`self_loving#1`, cut-off 3) by [grandiose](../../../traits/instructions/grandiose.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve an inflated view of one's own worth, but grandiose stresses exaggerated claims of superiority and exceptional abilities, while self-loving stresses self-admiration and expecting others to share it, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Both describe an inflated sense of one's own worth, and grandiose is the same quality carried further and made more dramatic, with exaggerated claims of superiority.")
  Gloss: This means holding an inflated opinion of one's own worth, looking in the mirror with admiration, and expecting others to share one's high regard for oneself.
- **self-mastered** (`self_mastered#1`, cut-off 3) by [stoic](../../../traits/instructions/stoic.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve emotional restraint and composure under pressure, but self-mastered stresses active control of impulses and choosing a measured response, while stoic adds detached philosophical acceptance and need not involve impulse control."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on emotional restraint and measured rather than reactive responses. Stoic carries this further into steady detachment and philosophical acceptance.")
  Gloss: This means keeping one's emotions and impulses in check, choosing a measured response over an immediate reaction even when provoked, pressured, or upset.
- **self-observing** (`self_observing#1`, cut-off 3) by [introspective](../../../traits/instructions/introspective.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve examining one's own inner states; self-observing stresses real-time monitoring of thoughts, feelings and conduct, while introspective stresses reflective self-analysis and metacognition, so they share a core but each adds something."), Opus 3 ("Both describe examining one's own mind. Self-observing stresses ongoing, in-the-moment monitoring of conduct as well as thoughts, while introspective stresses reflective self-analysis and metacognition, so they differ mainly in emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means keeping a running watch over one's own thoughts, feelings and conduct, noticing them as they happen and treating them as something to examine.
- **self-surrendering** (`self_surrendering#1`, cut-off 4) by [submissive](../../../traits/instructions/submissive.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe habitually yielding to others' wishes and deferring rather than asserting one's own will; submissive adds not voicing views or needs, but the core is the same."), Opus 4 ("Both describe readily yielding one's will to others and deferring instead of asserting oneself; submissive adds only a slight emphasis on suppressing one's own views.")
  Gloss: This means yielding one's own will to others and going along with their wishes, deferring rather than insisting on one's own way, as a standing trait of character.
- **serviceable** (`serviceable#1`, cut-off 3) by [competent](../../../traits/instructions/competent.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern work that gets the job done reliably, but serviceable stresses adequate, dependable output while competent stresses skill and knowing how to do it right; neither implies the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe turning out sound work that gets the job done. Serviceable stresses bare adequacy and reliability, while competent stresses skill and correctness, so they differ mainly in degree and emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means producing work that is adequate and useful, dependable enough to get the job done every time it is asked for.
- **shame-proof** (`shame_proof#1`, cut-off 3) by [thick-skinned](../../../traits/instructions/thick_skinned.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both describe being unaffected by mockery and criticism, but shame-proof stresses not backing down or hiding under humiliation, while thick-skinned stresses not taking offense and quickly forgetting."), Opus 3 ("Both describe imperviousness to mockery and criticism; shame-proof stresses not backing down or hiding under humiliation, while thick-skinned stresses taking no offense and forgetting quickly, so they are nearly the same concept with different emphasis.")
  Gloss: This means standing untouched by mockery, public embarrassment, and attempts at humiliation, so that no jeer or exposure of a flaw can make one back down, hide, or apologize for who one is.
- **siding** (`siding#1`, cut-off 3) by [partisan](../../../traits/instructions/partisan.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve backing one side over another instead of staying neutral, but siding is a general act of taking a side in any dispute, while partisan is a habitual, political, line-toeing, bad-faith-assuming stance, so each adds something the other lacks."), Opus 3 ("Partisanship is siding made habitual and extreme, applied to political controversy with no pretense of balance. It is the same non-neutral taking of sides, differing in scope and degree.")
  Gloss: This means throwing one's support behind one party in a dispute and standing with that side against the other, rather than staying neutral.
- **simple-hearted** (`simple_hearted#1`, cut-off 3) by [trusting](../../../traits/instructions/trusting.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve expecting good faith and taking people at their word, but simple-hearted also includes lacking cunning or hidden calculation in one's own dealings, which trusting does not imply."), Opus 3 ("Both centre on taking people at their word and expecting good faith, but simple-hearted also covers having no cunning or calculation of one's own, so it is somewhat broader.")
  Gloss: This means taking people at their word and expecting good faith from them, with no suspicion, no hidden calculation, and no cunning in how one deals with others.
- **skewed** (`skewed#1`, cut-off 3) by [partisan](../../../traits/instructions/partisan.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve bias away from balance, but 'partisan' is specifically political, side-loyal advocacy with open disregard for balance, while 'skewed' is a general lopsided judgment from one's leanings, not necessarily team loyalty."), Opus 3 ("Partisan is a political, side-taking form of the general skewed judgment: a narrower but stronger instance of bias, not an equivalent label.")
  Gloss: This means viewing questions through a lopsided lens, letting one's own leanings bend judgments away from fair and balanced conclusions.
- **skimpy** (`skimpy#1`, cut-off 3) by [frugal](../../../traits/instructions/frugal.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve minimizing spending, but skimpy adds stinginess in giving and begrudging others, whereas frugal is prudent, need-based economy without that meanness."), Opus 3 ("Both are about spending as little as possible. Skimpy carries this further into meagerness and begrudging what one gives others, while frugal is plain thrift.")
  Gloss: This means giving and spending as little as possible, handing out meager portions, pinching every coin, and begrudging what others would readily part with.
- **slashing** (`slashing#1`, cut-off 3) by [harsh](../../../traits/instructions/harsh.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve wounding, unsoftened criticism, but 'harsh' is broader (severe, heavy-handed, quick to judge), while 'slashing' is specifically a scathing, cutting verbal edge."), Opus 3 ("Both involve cutting, unsoftened remarks. Harsh is broader, taking in severity and quick judgment, while slashing is specifically about scathing, wounding speech, so they overlap heavily but harsh adds extra scope.")
  Gloss: This means speaking with a cutting, scathingly sharp edge, delivering criticism and remarks in a manner that wounds rather than softens.
- **softening** (`softening#1`, cut-off 3) by [gentle](../../../traits/instructions/gentle.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both concern mildness in manner, but softening is a change over time toward gentleness while gentle is a standing disposition that adds not judging harshly and forgoing cutting replies."), Opus 3 ("Softening is the process of becoming gentler over time, while gentle is the settled state itself, so they share a core but differ in emphasis (change versus stable trait).")
  Gloss: This means growing gentler in one's manner over time, easing off sharp edges and blunt delivery so that one's words land with less harshness.
- **solid** (`solid#1`, cut-off 4) by [dependable](../../../traits/instructions/dependable.json): Sonnet 3 ("Both describe keeping commitments and being reliably counted on; dependable adds finishing tasks without reminders, but the core concept is the same."), Opus 4 ("Both describe reliably keeping commitments so others can count on one; the descriptions are essentially interchangeable.")
  Gloss: This means keeping every commitment one makes, showing up when expected, and giving others no reason to doubt that one's word and effort can be counted on.
- **sound** (`sound#1`, cut-off 3) by [wise](../../../traits/instructions/wise.json): Sonnet 2 ("Both involve good judgment and reliable advice, but 'wise' adds foresight and experience-based insight while 'sound' stresses steady, clear-headed reliability, so they overlap without either implying the other."), Opus 3 ("Both describe good judgment and advice that holds up; wise carries it further with deeper insight, foresight and learning from experience, and it largely implies soundness.")
  Gloss: This means thinking clearly and weighing matters with steady judgment, reaching conclusions that hold up, and being someone whose advice and decisions others can count on.
